=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
There is a grid consisting of N cells numbered 1 to N.
Initially, cell i (1 \le i \le N) has an integer i \bmod 2 written in it. You can perform the following operation any number of times, possibly zero:

- Choose cells l and r (l+1 < r) that satisfy the following conditions, and replace each of the integers written in cells l+1, l+2, \dots, r-1 with the integer written in cell l.
- The integer written in cell l is equal to the integer written in cell r.
- The integer written in cell i (l < i < r) is different from the integer written in cell l.



Find the number, modulo 998244353, of sequences of operations that result in the integers written in cell i (1 \leq i \leq N) being A_i.
Two sequences of operations are considered different if and only if their lengths are different or there exists a positive integer t not exceeding the length of the sequences such that the (l, r) chosen in the t-th operations differ.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N
A_1 A_2 \dots A_N

Output

Print the answer.

Constraints


- 1 \leq N \leq 2 \times 10^5
- 0 \leq A_i \leq 1

Sample Input 1

6
1 1 1 1 1 0

Sample Output 1

3

To make the integers written in each cell i equal to A_i, for example, you can perform the following operations. (Here, we represent the state of the grid as a sequence X = (X_1, X_2, \dots, X_N).)

- Initially, X = (1, 0, 1, 0, 1, 0).
- Choose cells 2 and 4. X becomes (1, 0, 0, 0, 1, 0).
- Choose cells 1 and 5. X becomes (1, 1, 1, 1, 1, 0).

Besides the above, there are two other sequences of operations that result in the integers written in cell i being A_i, so the answer is 3.

Sample Input 2

10
1 1 1 1 1 0 1 1 1 0

Sample Output 2

9

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python

```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

This process of reducing an alternating sequence of odd length to one run by deleting interior runs. This is equivalent to choosing a full binary tree? Let's find recurrence.

Consider the last operation in a sequence that reduces segment of length L to one run. Before last operation, the sequence must have exactly 3 runs: c, 1-c, c. The last operation merges them. If we fix the last operation (which merges the three blocks), the operations before it consist of interleaved sequences reducing each block to one run. The total number of prior operations = (a-1)/2 + (b-1)/2 + (d-1)/2 = (L-3)/2. For each block j, there are f(length) sequences. They can be interleaved arbitrarily. Thus number of full sequences with given last split = multinomial coefficient * f(a) f(b) f(d). Then sum over all odd a,b,d positive summing L. This gives recurrence:

f(L) = sum_{a,b,d odd >=1, a+b+d=L} [ ( (L-3)/2 )! / ( ((a-1)/2)! ((b-1)/2)! ((d-1)/2)! ) ] * f(a) f(b) f(d).

Let's write solve():

import sys
MOD=998244353
def solve():
    data=list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    if not data: return
    N=data[0]; A=data[1:1+N]
    # Check A1
    if A[0] != 1:
        print(0); return
    # Compute runs lengths
    runs=[]
    cur=1
    for i in range(1,N):
        if A[i]==A[i-1]:
            cur+=1
        else:
            runs.append(cur)
            cur=1
    runs.append(cur)
    # Check all run lengths odd
    for L in runs:
        if L % 2 == 0:
            print(0); return
    # Since A1=1 and runs alternate, colors ok. But what if A contains values other than 0/1? constraints yes.
    m=len(runs)
    k=(N-m)//2  # integer
    # Precompute fact up to k? Need invfact up to k. N up to 2e5.
    fact=[1]*(k+1)
    for i in range(1,k+1):
        fact[i]=fact[i-1]*i%MOD
    invfact=[1]*(k+1)
    invfact[k]=pow(fact[k], MOD-2, MOD)
    for i in range(k,0,-1):
        invfact[i-1]=invfact[i]*i%MOD
    # Precompute df up to max k_j
    maxk=max((L-1)//2 for L in runs) if runs else 0
    df=[1]*(maxk+1)
    for i in range(1,maxk+1):
        df[i]=df[i-1]*(2*i-1)%MOD
    ans=fact[k]
    for L in runs:
        kj=(L-1)//2
        ans = ans * df[kj] % MOD
        ans = ans * invfact[kj] % MOD
    print(ans)

Need ensure fact length k+1; if k=0, invfact[0]=pow(1,...)=1, loop okay. maxk maybe 0. Good.

This boundary deletion view may yield a general formula: Starting with N-1 boundaries, delete adjacent pairs, leave final boundaries. The number of sequences maybe product over gaps of double factorial times multinomial? Let's see. Final boundaries split initial boundaries into gaps: before first final boundary, between final boundaries, after last final boundary. The boundaries that are deleted within each gap? Actually final boundaries survive; all other boundaries are deleted. Operations can delete pairs that may straddle a final boundary? Thus deletions are confined to intervals between final boundaries (including ends), where the surviving final boundaries act as barriers. Within each gap of consecutive initial boundaries between two surviving boundaries (or ends), we need delete all boundaries in that gap by adjacent-pair deletions, without touching the barriers. This is analogous to reducing a segment of length? Let's map to our run formula.

Initial boundaries positions 1..N-1. Final boundaries B_j = cumulative run lengths. The gaps:

Let's derive the interleaving count more formally:
For each final run j, there are k_j operations internal to that run. A global sequence is a shuffle of these internal sequences. The number of shuffles of sequences of lengths k_j is multinomial k! / ∏ k_j!. Since internal sequences are ordered, and operations from different runs are distinguishable by their intervals. Yes.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue
</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
import sys

MOD = 998244353

def solve() -> None:
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    if not data:
        return

    N = data[0]
    A = data[1:1 + N]

    # Cell 1 never changes.
    if A[0] != 1:
        print(0)
        return

    # Extract run lengths.
    runs = []
    cur = 1
    for i in range(1, N):
        if A[i] == A[i - 1]:
            cur += 1
        else:
            runs.append(cur)
            cur = 1
    runs.append(cur)

    # Every final run must have odd length.
    for L in runs:
        if L % 2 == 0:
            print(0)
            return

    m = len(runs)
    k = (N - m) // 2

    # factorials
    fact = [1] * (k + 1)
    for i in range(1, k + 1):
        fact[i] = fact[i - 1] * i % MOD

    invfact = [1] * (k + 1)
    invfact[k] = pow(fact[k], MOD - 2, MOD)
    for i in range(k, 0, -1):
        invfact[i - 1] = invfact[i] * i % MOD

    # df[x] = (2x-1)!!
    max_k = max((L - 1) // 2 for L in runs)
    df = [1] * (max_k + 1)
    for i in range(1, max_k + 1):
        df[i] = df[i - 1] * (2 * i - 1) % MOD

    ans = fact[k]
    for L in runs:
        x = (L - 1) // 2
        ans = ans * df[x] % MOD
        ans = ans * invfact[x] % MOD

    print(ans)


if __name__ == "__main__":
    solve()
```
